<sup>2</sup>, <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Bonura%20G"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id8"><span class="name western">Giuseppe Bonura</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id8">

<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2025 Oct 15; Revised 2025 Nov 12; Accepted 2025 Nov 18; Collection date 2025 Dec.</p></section></div></div>

This article is an open access article distributed under the terms and conditions of the Creative Commons Attribution (CC BY) license (<a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/</a>).</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>

<div>PMCID: PMC12693164  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41369429/" class="usa-link">41369429</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>The lifetime of polymer electrolyte membrane fuel cells (PEMFCs) is significantly influenced by the degradation of their catalysts. A composite-type electrocatalyst support with the formula Ti<sub>(1−x)</sub>Mo<sub>x</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C (x: 0–0.2, C: carbon) has been found to provide higher stability for the Pt active metal than carbon alone. Non-traditional carbon materials such as graphene nanoplatelets (GNPs) and graphite oxide (GO) offer new possibilities for supports. This work aims to explore whether it is possible to combine the advantageous properties of GNP and GO in composite-supported Pt electrocatalysts. Composites prepared using the modified sol–gel method and Pt catalysts supported on them were characterized by physicochemical methods. Electrochemical behavior in terms of CO tolerance, activity and stability was studied. Although GO transformed into a mainly graphitic material during composite synthesis, its addition still increased the functional group content of the carbonaceous backbone. The electrical conductivity was significantly higher when GNPs-GO mixtures were used as the starting carbon material compared to the use of pure GNPs. Increased CO oxidation activity was achieved due to the incorporated Mo. Stability of the composite-supported Pt catalyst was significantly higher than that of commercial Pt/C. Increased stability of the GNPs-GO-derived catalyst compared to the GNP-derived one was obtained.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> electrocatalyst, mixed oxide-carbon composites, sol–gel method, graphene nanoplatelets (GNPs), GNPs-graphite oxide (GO) mixture, XRD, TG-MS, TEM, SEM, XPS</p></section></section><section id="sec1-nanomaterials-15-01753"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>Polymer electrolyte membrane fuel cells (PEMFCs) convert chemical energy into electricity efficiently and in an environmentally friendly way, and their use provides advantages in a wide variety of areas [<a href="#B1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-nanomaterials-15-01753">1</a>,<a href="#B2-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-nanomaterials-15-01753">2</a>,<a href="#B3-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-nanomaterials-15-01753">3</a>,<a href="#B4-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-nanomaterials-15-01753">4</a>]. The lifetime of PEMFCs is significantly influenced by the degradation of the catalyst used in them [<a href="#B5-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5-nanomaterials-15-01753">5</a>,<a href="#B6-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6-nanomaterials-15-01753">6</a>,<a href="#B7-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-nanomaterials-15-01753">7</a>]. Due to its activity both in the oxygen reduction reaction (ORR) on the cathode and in the hydrogen oxidation reaction (HOR) on the anode side, nano-dispersed platinum is a favorable electrocatalyst [<a href="#B6-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6-nanomaterials-15-01753">6</a>,<a href="#B8-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-nanomaterials-15-01753">8</a>]. Likewise, due to its excellent conductivity, carbon is a suitable catalyst support. As a consequence, carbon-supported platinum (Pt/C) is a widely used electrocatalyst for commercial PEMFC; however, Pt/C is prone to electrocorrosion [<a href="#B9-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-nanomaterials-15-01753">9</a>,<a href="#B10-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-nanomaterials-15-01753">10</a>,<a href="#B11-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-nanomaterials-15-01753">11</a>] and is not resistant to CO poisoning [<a href="#B8-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-nanomaterials-15-01753">8</a>,<a href="#B12-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-nanomaterials-15-01753">12</a>]. One of the reasons behind electrocorrosion is that carbon is not thermodynamically stable under the operating conditions in PEMFCs [<a href="#B13-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-nanomaterials-15-01753">13</a>]. The effect of catalyst degradation is offset by applying high Pt loading to the electrodes, which improves the lifetime of the PEMFC to an acceptable level. Since Pt is one of the critical and strategic raw materials [<a href="#B14-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-nanomaterials-15-01753">14</a>,<a href="#B15-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-nanomaterials-15-01753">15</a>,<a href="#B16-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-nanomaterials-15-01753">16</a>], the development of Pt electrocatalysts with increased stability contributing to lower Pt need has great importance.</p>
<p>In our previous works, we found that composite-type catalyst supports with the formula Ti<sub>(1−x)</sub>Mo<sub>x</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C (x: 0–0.2, C: Black Pearls 2000, Vulcan XC-72 (Cabot)) provided higher stability for the Pt active metal than simple conductive carbon supports [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>]. Every element of the above composite has an important function: the TiO<sub>2</sub> skeleton being strongly resistant to electrocorrosion increases the stability and hinders the dissolution of the incorporated dopant; the Mo doping ions act as a co-catalyst providing enhanced CO oxidation activity and CO tolerance; and the carbon material provides appropriate specific surface area (SSA<sub>BET</sub>) and electrical conductivity [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>,<a href="#B18-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-nanomaterials-15-01753">18</a>]. We have already conducted some preliminary studies to produce this type of catalyst support from non-traditional carbon materials such as graphite oxide (GO) [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>,<a href="#B18-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-nanomaterials-15-01753">18</a>] and graphene nanoplatelets (GNPs) [<a href="#B18-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-nanomaterials-15-01753">18</a>].</p>
<p>Graphene-based, non-traditional carbon materials such as carbon nanotubes (CNT) and graphene nanosheets [<a href="#B19-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-nanomaterials-15-01753">19</a>,<a href="#B20-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-nanomaterials-15-01753">20</a>,<a href="#B21-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-nanomaterials-15-01753">21</a>,<a href="#B22-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-nanomaterials-15-01753">22</a>,<a href="#B23-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-nanomaterials-15-01753">23</a>,<a href="#B24-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-nanomaterials-15-01753">24</a>,<a href="#B25-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25-nanomaterials-15-01753">25</a>,<a href="#B26-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26-nanomaterials-15-01753">26</a>,<a href="#B27-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-nanomaterials-15-01753">27</a>] offer new possibilities for electrocatalyst support, but certain functionalization is required for their use [<a href="#B28-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-nanomaterials-15-01753">28</a>,<a href="#B29-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-nanomaterials-15-01753">29</a>]. The functionalization of the carbonaceous materials is especially important when preparing their composites with inorganic materials [<a href="#B30-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-nanomaterials-15-01753">30</a>,<a href="#B31-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-nanomaterials-15-01753">31</a>]. The method of modification strongly depends on the type of the graphene materials, since the family of graphene derivatives is rather extended [<a href="#B32-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-nanomaterials-15-01753">32</a>]. They are classified both according to their functional groups and their size range [<a href="#B32-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-nanomaterials-15-01753">32</a>,<a href="#B33-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-nanomaterials-15-01753">33</a>]. GO is known for a long time [<a href="#B34-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-nanomaterials-15-01753">34</a>,<a href="#B35-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-nanomaterials-15-01753">35</a>] and is now popularly used as a starting material for various syntheses [<a href="#B23-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-nanomaterials-15-01753">23</a>,<a href="#B36-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-nanomaterials-15-01753">36</a>]. Delaminated GO, which is actually graphene oxide, can be considered “as derivatized graphene with a myriad of oxygen functionalities due to the introduction of carbonyl, hydroxyl, and epoxy groups on the planar surfaces and edges of the carbon sheets” [<a href="#B33-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-nanomaterials-15-01753">33</a>]. The specific structure of GO results in its thermal instability [<a href="#B37-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-nanomaterials-15-01753">37</a>], weak acidity in water and more hydrophilic character [<a href="#B38-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-nanomaterials-15-01753">38</a>]. Its amphiphilic character makes it suitable for easy use in aqueous syntheses [<a href="#B36-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-nanomaterials-15-01753">36</a>]. Nevertheless, non-oxidized aromatic regions and/or isolated C = C double bonds are also characteristic of the structure of GO [<a href="#B37-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-nanomaterials-15-01753">37</a>]. However, GO is relatively expensive, and harmful waste materials are formed during its production. In contrast, the other commonly used “graphene” material, GNP, is relatively cheap and commercially available in large quantities [<a href="#B39-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-nanomaterials-15-01753">39</a>,<a href="#B40-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-nanomaterials-15-01753">40</a>,<a href="#B41-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-nanomaterials-15-01753">41</a>]. There is increasing interest in the use of GNP as an electrocatalyst support [<a href="#B42-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-nanomaterials-15-01753">42</a>,<a href="#B43-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-nanomaterials-15-01753">43</a>,<a href="#B44-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-nanomaterials-15-01753">44</a>,<a href="#B45-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-nanomaterials-15-01753">45</a>,<a href="#B46-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-nanomaterials-15-01753">46</a>]. Due to its relatively extensive condensed aromatic ring system, the thermal and electrical conductivity of GNP is excellent [<a href="#B47-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-nanomaterials-15-01753">47</a>]. However, GNPs are much less hydrophilic than GO, and the amount of O-containing functional groups on them is insufficient, as they are positioned only on the edges of the nanoparticles [<a href="#B48-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-nanomaterials-15-01753">48</a>]. The latter is not particularly advantageous for preparing composites in which cohesive forces are based on covalent bonds, such as composites of inorganic components synthesized via the sol–gel method.</p>
<p>Nevertheless, other interactions besides covalent bonds may also play a decisive role in carbonaceous–carbonaceous composites. The π-π* interaction is supposed to be responsible for the formation of graphene–CNT hybrid materials [<a href="#B49-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-nanomaterials-15-01753">49</a>] and composites of CNT- hyperbranched polyesters with terminal functional groups [<a href="#B50-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-nanomaterials-15-01753">50</a>]. π-π bond-based, so-called non-covalent functionalization was used to create an N-type anchoring site for Pt by the introduction of electron-conducting polyaniline (PANI) onto the surface of single wall nanotubes (SWCNTs) [<a href="#B51-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-nanomaterials-15-01753">51</a>]. Non-covalent functionalization with amphiphilic polymers resulted in very well-dispersed multi-walled carbon nanotubes (MWNTs) in a solvent of dimethylformamide, water and chloroform [<a href="#B52-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-nanomaterials-15-01753">52</a>]. In another work, phenyl glycidyl ether molecules were loaded onto MWCNTs based on π-π* interaction, which increased the dispersibility of MWCNTs in polyamide nanocomposite fibers [<a href="#B53-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-nanomaterials-15-01753">53</a>]. A hexaazatrinaphthalene-conductive reduced graphene oxide (rGO) composite for aqueous proton storage was successfully prepared, in which π-π interaction existed [<a href="#B54-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-nanomaterials-15-01753">54</a>]. GO has been described to serve as a superior dispersant to disperse pristine CNTs [<a href="#B55-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-nanomaterials-15-01753">55</a>] or GNPs [<a href="#B48-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-nanomaterials-15-01753">48</a>,<a href="#B56-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-nanomaterials-15-01753">56</a>] into water to form stable suspensions. These observations can be attributed to interactions between the carbonaceous sheets of the different graphene derivatives, including π-π attraction [<a href="#B48-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-nanomaterials-15-01753">48</a>,<a href="#B56-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-nanomaterials-15-01753">56</a>], as well as to the hydrophilic nature of GO, due to its O-containing functional groups.</p>
<p>This work aimed to explore whether it is possible to combine the advantageous properties of GNPs and GO in the catalyst production method we developed. Therefore, we studied the effect of mixtures of GNPs and GO on the behavior of the mixed oxide–carbon composite-supported electrocatalyst. A further question is whether the presence of GO during the preparation of the composite results in a more stable catalyst than pure GNPs. Finally, could the properties of the new catalysts indicate a GO-GNP interaction; can we assume non-covalent functionalization of GNPs by GO? In order to investigate the utilization of advanced carbon materials, a series of Ti<sub>(1−x)</sub>Mo<sub>x</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C composite-type supports and supported Pt electrocatalysts were prepared using GNPs or GO-GNP mixtures (3:1, 1:1, 1:3) as starting carbon-containing materials. To the best of our knowledge, preparation of a mixed oxide–carbon composite-type electrocatalyst support from GNP-GO carbonaceous components, as well as the changes in the carbonaceous part during the composite formation, are reported here for the first time in the literature. The new types of support materials and the catalysts prepared by loading them with 20 wt.% Pt are compared based on their physicochemical properties and electrochemical behavior.</p></section><section id="sec2-nanomaterials-15-01753"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Materials and Methods</h2>
<section id="sec2dot1-nanomaterials-15-01753"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Materials</h3>
<p>Three different commercial GNPs (Nanografi, Ankara, Turkey, specific surface area: 700 m<sup>2</sup>/g; Sigma-Aldrich No900394 (Sigma-Aldrich, St. Louis, MO, USA), average surface area: 300 m<sup>2</sup>/g; Sigma-Aldrich No900407 (Sigma-Aldrich, St. Louis, MO, USA), average surface area: 750 m<sup>2</sup>/g) and homemade GO were used as carbon precursors for composite preparation. GO suspension (0.95 wt.% for carbon) was prepared by the Hummers–Offeman technique [<a href="#B35-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-nanomaterials-15-01753">35</a>]. The Ti and Mo precursor compounds were titanium-isopropoxide (Ti(O-<em>i</em>-Pr)<sub>4</sub>, Sigma-Aldrich, St. Louis, MO, USA, 97%) and ammonium heptamolybdate tetrahydrate ((NH<sub>4</sub>)<sub>6</sub>Mo<sub>7</sub>O<sub>24</sub> × 4H<sub>2</sub>O, Merck, Darmstadt, Germany, 99%). The Pt precursor was hexachloroplatinic acid hexahydrate (H<sub>2</sub>PtCl<sub>6</sub> × 6H<sub>2</sub>O, Sigma-Aldrich, St. Louis, MO, USA, 37.5% Pt), and 20 wt.% Pt/Vulcan XC-72 (Quintech, Göppingen, Germany) was used as reference Pt/C electrocatalyst. Nitric acid (HNO<sub>3</sub>, 65%, a.r.), 2-propanol (<em>i</em>-C<sub>3</sub>H<sub>5</sub>OH, 99.9 <em>V</em>/<em>V</em>%, a.r.) ethylene-glycol (EG, 99.8%) and sodium borohydride (NaBH<sub>4</sub>, 99.95%) were purchased from Molar Chemicals, Halásztelek, Hungary. Sulfuric acid (H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub>, 96% p.a., Merck, Darmstadt, Germany) and hydrochloric acid (HCl, 36,4%, AnalaR NORMAPUR, VWR International, Fontenay-sous-Bois, France) were also used. Sodium hydroxide (NaOH, &gt;98%) and hydrofluoric acid (38–41%, a.r.) were Reanal (Budapest, Hungary) products. The gases (Ar, N<sub>2</sub>, H<sub>2</sub>) used in this work were products of Linde Gáz Magyarország Zrt. (Budapest, Hungary) with 5.0 purity. Catalyst ink was prepared using a 5% Nafion<sup>®</sup> dispersion (DuPont™ Nafion<sup>®</sup> PFSA Polymer Dispersions DE 520, DuPont, Wilmington, DE, USA).</p></section><section id="sec2dot2-nanomaterials-15-01753"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Preparation of Composite Supports and Supported Pt Electrocatalysts</h3>
<p>Denomination of the composite materials used as electrocatalyst supports is shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>. Synthesis of the composites started with preparation of the mixture of carbonaceous components either from GNPs and water or from GO sol, GNPs and water. It is important to mention that due to the highly apolar nature of GNPs, intensive sonication by a Hielscher UP200S ultrasonic device (Hielscher Ultrasonics GmbH, Teltow, Germany) was used. In the case of the GO component, the pH of the mixtures was adjusted to 9 with concentrated NaOH solution to reach more intense exfoliation. In parallel, we started the formation of TiO<sub>2</sub> nuclei from Ti(O-<em>i</em>-Pr)<sub>4</sub> in acidic sol (5 h), and then the two mixtures were combined according to the sol–gel based multi-step synthesis method optimized in our previous works [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>,<a href="#B57-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-nanomaterials-15-01753">57</a>]. This procedure involves three separate steps: (i) formation and aging of TiO<sub>2</sub> nuclei on the carbonaceous material (6 days), (ii) addition of Mo-precursor and drying at low temperature (overnight at 85 °C), and (iii) high-temperature heat treatment (HTT) in Ar at 600 °C for the crystallization of mixed oxide and Mo incorporation.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-t001"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h4>
<div class="caption p"><p>Denomination and preparation details of Ti<sub>(1−x)</sub>Mo<sub>x</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C (C = GNP, GNP-GO derived carbonaceous material) composites. Nominal composition: 75 wt.% Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-25 wt.% C.</p></div>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/nanomaterials-15-01753-t001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn2"><p><sup>1</sup> The amount of GO was calculated according to the C<sub>8</sub>O<sub>4</sub>H<sub>2</sub> general formula of GO. <sup>2</sup> Ti and Mo precursor compounds: Ti(O-i-Pr)<sub>4</sub> and (NH<sub>4</sub>)<sub>6</sub>Mo<sub>7</sub>O<sub>24</sub> × 4H<sub>2</sub>O. <sup>3</sup> cc. HNO<sub>3</sub> (65%, Molar Chemicals, a.r.). <sup>4</sup> pH adjusted to 9 with concentrated NaOH solution. <sup>5</sup> concentration of GO in the sol. <sup>6</sup> Nanografi, specific surface area = 700 m<sup>2</sup>/g. <sup>7</sup> Sigma-Aldrich No900394, average surface area = 300 m<sup>2</sup>/g. <sup>8</sup> Sigma-Aldrich No900407, average surface area = 750 m<sup>2</sup>/g. <sup>9</sup> NaNO<sub>3</sub> removal (washing step) before adding Mo precursor.</p></div></div></section><p>Details of the present composite preparation method can be found in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a> (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Section S1.1 and Figures S1 and S2</a>). In this work we aimed to prepare the mixed oxide part of the new composites from GNPs and from GNP-GO mixtures with the same nominal compositions as we used previously; therefore, the oxide/carbon mass ratio of 75/25 and Ti/Mo atomic ratio of 80/20 were chosen [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>,<a href="#B57-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-nanomaterials-15-01753">57</a>]. We kept the Ti/Mo ratio relatively low, as we previously found that at doping levels above 20–30%, the Mo dopant incorporated incompletely into the mixed oxide, and an undesirable, separate MoO<sub>2</sub> phase appeared. Another prerequisite of the exclusive Mo incorporation into the mixed oxide is the formation of a rutile-TiO<sub>2</sub> phase [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>]. Therefore, composite formation and aging of TiO<sub>2</sub> nuclei on the carbonaceous material were carried out in diluted HNO<sub>3</sub> solution.</p>
<p>It should be mentioned that in the case of the GO-containing samples, prior to Mo introduction, a washing step with HNO<sub>3</sub> was included in order to avoid harmful NaNO<sub>3</sub> formation [<a href="#B18-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-nanomaterials-15-01753">18</a>]. Further synthesis parameters of the composites are summarized in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>.</p>
<p>To obtain platinum-containing electrocatalysts, the Ti<sub>(1×x)</sub>Mo<sub>x</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C supports were loaded with 20 wt.% Pt by a modified, NaBH<sub>4</sub>-assisted EG reduction–precipitation technique, as we described previously [<a href="#B58-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-nanomaterials-15-01753">58</a>], demonstrated in <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S3</a> (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Section S1 of the Supplementary Materials</a>).</p></section><section id="sec2dot3-nanomaterials-15-01753"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Physicochemical Characterization of Composite Supports and Supported Pt Electrocatalysts</h3>
<p>Details of the nitrogen physisorption measurements, transmission electron microscopy (TEM) and scanning electron microscopy (SEM) studies, inductively coupled plasma-optical emission spectrometry (ICP-OES), X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy (XPS) and X-ray powder diffraction (XRD) measurements are described in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials (Section S2.1)</a>. The average crystallite sizes of Pt and mixed oxide were determined from XRD data by means of the Scherrer formula [<a href="#B59-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-nanomaterials-15-01753">59</a>,<a href="#B60-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-nanomaterials-15-01753">60</a>].</p>
<p>The surface morphology of the samples was investigated using an Ultra-High-Resolution Scanning Electron Microscope (UHR-SEM-FEG, Helios 5 UC DualBeam, Thermo Scientific, Waltham, MA, USA) equipped with a Field Emission Gun (FEG), which provides high spatial resolution and improved image quality.</p>
<p>The simultaneous thermogravimetric (TG) and mass spectrometric (MS) evolved gas analyses were recorded on a Setaram LabsysEvo (Setaram, Lyon, France) thermal analyzer, in high purity (99.9999%) He, with a flow rate of 90 cm<sup>3</sup>/min. The measurements were performed with a heating rate of 20 °C/min, in the temperature range of 25–1000 °C. Samples were weighed without further treatment in 100 μL alumina crucibles. The results were baseline-corrected, and then evaluated using the thermal analyzer’s processing software (AKTS Calisto Processing, ver. 2.15). In parallel with the TG measurements, the analysis of the evolved gas was carried out on a Pfeiffer Vacuum OmniStar™ (Pfeiffer Vacuum, Asslar, Germany) gas analysis system. The gas splitter and transfer line to the mass spectrometer was preheated to 200 °C. The scanned <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> interval was 11–80 amu, with a scan speed of 50 ms amu<sup>−1</sup>. The mass spectrometer was operated in electron impact mode.</p>
<p>The conductivity of the powdered electrocatalysts was assessed using a home-made device in a two-electrode cell arrangement (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S4</a> in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a>). Details of the method are shown elsewhere [<a href="#B18-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-nanomaterials-15-01753">18</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot4-nanomaterials-15-01753"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Electrochemical Characterization</h3>
<p>The electrochemical measurements were performed using standard three-electrode electrochemical cell and a Biologic SP 150 potentiostat (BioLogic, Seyssinet-Pariset, France). The electrolyte was 0.5 M H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> solution prepared from Milli-Q water and concentrated H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub>. The working electrode was made of glassy carbon (GC; d = 0.3 cm) and had a surface area of 0.0707 cm<sup>2</sup>. A hydrogen electrode was used as reference electrode, while a platinum wire was used as counter electrode. All potentials are given on reversible hydrogen electrode (RHE) scale. The detailed description of the working electrode preparation, the catalyst ink composition and electrocatalytic measurements can be found in Refs. [<a href="#B58-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-nanomaterials-15-01753">58</a>,<a href="#B61-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-nanomaterials-15-01753">61</a>]. The Pt loading of working electrodes was 10 µg cm<sup>−2</sup>.</p>
<p>Cyclic voltammetry, CO<sub>ads</sub>-stripping voltammetry and short-term (500-cycle) and long-term (10,000-cycle) stability tests were performed to calculate the electrochemically active Pt surface area (ECSA), to investigate the CO oxidation activity and to assess the stability of the 20 wt.% Pt/Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C electrocatalysts. Linear sweep voltammetry tests were performed on rotating disk electrodes (RDEs) in order to investigate the ORR activity. Further details are described in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials (Section S2.2)</a>.</p>
<p>The electrochemical performance of the newly developed electrocatalysts, involving activity in ORR and long-term stability, was compared with that of commercial 20 wt.% Pt/C (Quintech).</p></section></section><section id="sec3-nanomaterials-15-01753"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Results</h2>
<section id="sec3dot1-nanomaterials-15-01753"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Preliminary Results Obtained by Use of GNPs from Different Sources</h3>
<p>Our previous results have shown that a relationship exists between the specific surface area of the parent carbonaceous material and that of the composite formed: the larger the surface area of the parent carbon, the larger the surface area of the composite [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>]. Moreover, if the carbon did not provide a surface large enough to accommodate TiO<sub>2</sub>, e.g., the TiO<sub>2</sub>/C ratio was too high, the desired Mo incorporation under the HTT was only marginal, and separate Mo-oxide phases were formed [<a href="#B62-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B62-nanomaterials-15-01753">62</a>]. Thus, it could be concluded that the carbon material behaves as a template during preparation of composites byour sol–gel based method. We have also found in our previous work that the SSA<sub>BET</sub> of the composite Ti<sub>(1−x)</sub>Mo<sub>x</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C (C: Black Pearls 2000, Vulcan XC-72) decreased significantly compared to the parent carbon material [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>]. This observation can be explained by pore blocking and/or partial filling of pores and spaces between particles during the formation of the TiO<sub>2</sub>-based mixed oxide. In fact, SSA<sub>BET</sub> is one of the metrics which is obtained on the base of physisorption measurements and it is strongly connected to the pore structure. In <a href="#sec2dot1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Section 2.1</a>, the surface area provided by the manufacturer was listed for the investigated commercially available GNPs. In addition, in a series of experiments, we also investigated the N<sub>2</sub> physisorption behavior of these GNP materials, in order to choose the appropriate parent carbon for further experiments. The main parameters of the various parent GNPs and the composites obtained from these measurements are summarized in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a> and <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Table S1</a>, <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figures S5–S7</a> in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a>.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-t002"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 2.</h4>
<div class="caption p"><p>Characterization of the composite catalyst supports prepared by GNPs from various sources (nominal value of Ti<sub>(1−x)</sub>Mo<sub>x</sub>O<sub>2</sub>/C = 75 wt.%/25 wt.%, nominal value of Ti/Mo = 4/1 at/at).</p></div>

</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/nanomaterials-15-01753-t002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn3"><p><sup>1</sup> SSA<sub>BET</sub> and total pore volume determined by low temperature N<sub>2</sub> physisorption measurements. <sup>2</sup> Average crystallite size calculated from Scherrer equation. <sup>3</sup> Lattice parameters of the rutile phase after HTT; Pure rutile TiO<sub>2</sub>: a = 4.593 Å, c <em>=</em> 2.959 Å. <sup>4</sup> From Ref. [<a href="#B18-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-nanomaterials-15-01753">18</a>].</p></div></div></section><p>Regarding the XRD patterns of parent GNPs (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S7A</a> in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a>), it can be seen that GNP-S1, which had lower SSA<sub>BET</sub> than the other two GNP materials, showed much higher intensity at around 2θ = 26° (graphite (002) reflection). This is in fact reasonable, since the higher crystallinity of GNP-S1, as proven by XRD, would lead to lower SSA<sub>BET</sub>. This high intensity peak attributed to the carbon is observed in the GNP-S1-based composite material (100GNP-S1) before (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S7B</a> line a) and after (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S7C</a> line a) the HTT, while such a peak was not detected for 100GNP-S2 and 100GNP-NG composites. XRD patterns of all the composites after the drying step at 85 °C proved the presence of rutile-TiO<sub>2</sub> nuclei (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S7B</a>). The intensities of the peaks characteristic of the mixed oxide part increased after HTT, which reveals that the crystallinity of the samples was improved by HTT (see <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S7C</a>). Although XRD results proved that the desired rutile phase was formed and no peak attributed to MoO<sub>2</sub> was obtained, demonstrating the successful incorporation for all supports (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S7C</a>), the synthesis process resulted in significant SSA<sub>BET</sub> drop, and the values of total pore volume were moderate (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>) in every case. The isotherms of all three 100GNP samples were similar to each other; they showed type IV isotherms according to the IUPAC classification [<a href="#B63-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63-nanomaterials-15-01753">63</a>] with a slight H3 type hysteresis loop, characteristic for slit-like mesopores with wide pore size distribution (see <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S6</a> in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a>). Compared to the parent GNP supports, the specific surface area significantly decreased due to the penetration of precipitated metal oxides into the mesopores and to some pore blocking effects. It can be seen from the data of <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a> that the SSA<sub>BET</sub> of all three composites was close to the recommended value required for electrocatalyst support materials (100 m<sup>2</sup>/g [<a href="#B64-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64-nanomaterials-15-01753">64</a>]).</p>
<p>Consistent with the idea of the role of carbon as a template, 100GNP-S2 has the highest SSA<sub>BET</sub>; consequently, GNP-S2 was chosen for further experiments.</p></section><section id="sec3dot2-nanomaterials-15-01753"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. Physicochemical Characterization of Composite Supports and Supported Pt Electrocatalysts</h3>
<section id="sec3dot2dot1-nanomaterials-15-01753"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.1. Characterization of Composite Type of Catalyst Supports</h4>
<p>Results of the N<sub>2</sub> physisorption experiments of the composite type of supports prepared from GNP-S2 and GO are summarized in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>; the isotherms are presented in <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S8 in the Section S3.2.1</a> of the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a>.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-t003"><h5 class="obj_head">Table 3.</h5>
<div class="caption p"><p>Characterization of the composite type of catalysts supports prepared from GNP-GO mixtures (nominal value of Ti/Mo = 4/1 at/at, nominal value of Ti<sub>(1−x)</sub>Mo<sub>x</sub>O<sub>2</sub>/C = 75 wt.%/25 wt.%).</p></div>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/nanomaterials-15-01753-t003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn4"><p><sup>1</sup> SSA<sub>BET</sub> and total pore volume determined by low temperature N<sub>2</sub> physisorption measurements. <sup>2</sup> Average crystallite size of mixed oxide calculated using the Scherrer equation.</p></div></div></section><p>The shapes of the isotherms are very similar to each other but slightly differ from that of pure GNP-originated ones (cf. <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figures S6 and S8</a> in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a>). According to IUPAC classification [<a href="#B63-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63-nanomaterials-15-01753">63</a>], the support materials have a type IV isotherm, with an H3/H4 type of hysteresis loop that denotes aggregated flat particles and reflects the structure of the parent carbonaceous material—that is, the graphene-oxide sheets of the delaminated GO. The specific surface area and total pore volume values are in the same range; no correlation was found between the GO or GNPs content and SSA<sub>BET</sub> or pore volume.</p>
<p>The changes in the oxide part of the composite during the preparation can be followed using the XRD measurements of the samples from the different stages of the modified sol–gel procedure (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-f001"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12693164_nanomaterials-15-01753-g001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/029a/12693164/9f6341089b24/nanomaterials-15-01753-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="576" width="778" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/nanomaterials-15-01753-f001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>XRD patterns of the composite samples from GNP-GO mixtures with various GNPs/GO ratios in the different stages of their preparation. (<strong>A</strong>) before HTT, (<strong>B</strong>) after HTT, (<strong>C</strong>) after Pt loading. a: 25GNP-S2, b: 50GNP-S2, c: 75GNP-S2, d: 100GNP-S2, ●—rutile. (<strong>D</strong>) Reflection positions for rutile TiO<sub>2</sub> (JCPDS card no. 21-1276) and Pt (JCPDS card no. 87-0647).</p></figcaption></figure><p>Rutile nuclei appeared in all samples during the aging step of TiO<sub>2</sub> with the carbonaceous material (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>A), which led to the predominant formation of rutile, i.e., crystalline titania-based phases detected by XRD contain exclusively rutile after the HTT, as we found in our previous works [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>,<a href="#B18-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-nanomaterials-15-01753">18</a>,<a href="#B58-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-nanomaterials-15-01753">58</a>]. No separate crystalline Mo-oxide could be observed. The calculated lattice parameters of the samples after the HTT were the same for all samples (a~4.63 Å, c~2.94 Å), which indicated approximately 18% Mo incorporation. The broad applicability of our synthesis method is demonstrated by the fact that the structural properties of the supports met the expectations. The average (primary) oxide particle size calculated from the XRD results was between 9 and 13 nm, and also showed no correlation with GO or GNPs content (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>). It should be noted that, according to our previous experience, the oxide phase exists in large, star-like aggregates and smaller units, although their ratio may vary significantly from sample to sample, depending on parameters such as SSA<sub>BET</sub> or functionalization of the carbon [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>].</p>
<p>The changes in the carbonaceous part of the composite during the preparation were followed by the simultaneous thermogravimetric (TG) and mass spectrometric (MS) evolved gas analyses performed on the samples from the different stages of the modified sol–gel procedure. The motivation for the above measurements is that GO is thermally unstable [<a href="#B65-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-nanomaterials-15-01753">65</a>]; their functional groups, which are known to be heat-sensitive, are gradually lost with increasing temperature [<a href="#B66-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-nanomaterials-15-01753">66</a>]. However, high-temperature treatment is required for the dopant element to be incorporated into the rutile-TiO<sub>2</sub> phase. The results of the thermal analysis are summarized in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a> and in <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Tables S2–S6</a> (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Section S3.2.2 in the Supplementary Materials</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-f002"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12693164_nanomaterials-15-01753-g002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/029a/12693164/e70965a69eca/nanomaterials-15-01753-g002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="698" width="725" alt="Figure 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/nanomaterials-15-01753-f002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Mass loss rate (DTG) and heat flow curves of Ti<sub>(1−x)</sub>Mo<sub>x</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C composites before and after HTT. Appropriate curves of parent GNP-S2 are given for comparison. (<strong>A</strong>) DTG curves of samples before HTT; (<strong>B</strong>) DTG curves of samples after HTT; (<strong>C</strong>) heat flow curves of samples before HTT; (<strong>D</strong>) Heat flow curves of samples after HTT; a: 75GNP-S2; b: 50GNP-S2; c: 25GNP-S2; d: 100GNP-S2; e: parent GNP-S2 as-received.</p></figcaption></figure><p>It can be seen that thermal changes in the composites before HTT can be divided into two main intervals, such as low-temperature changes (range up to~150 °C) and medium-temperature changes (range ~150–300 °C), both well-distinguishable in the case of samples obtained after the drying step at 80 °C (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>A,C). The resulting features are proportional to the GO content in both the DTG (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>A) and heat flow (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>C) measurements. Based on the TG-MS trace (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Section S3.2.2 in the Supplementary Materials</a>), it could be concluded that the low-temperature region of up to ~150 °C is related to the removal of adsorbed water. The parallel formation of H<sub>2</sub>O, CO<sub>2</sub>, CO, and formaldehyde (H<sub>2</sub>CO) between ~150–300 °C is due to partial removal of the oxygen-containing functional groups from the carbonaceous part of the composite, in line with the literature finding related to the decomposition of pure GO [<a href="#B66-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-nanomaterials-15-01753">66</a>]. Increasing GO content in the composite led to increasing mass loss rate and heat flow (exothermic) in the region of ~150–300 °C. Ref. [<a href="#B66-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-nanomaterials-15-01753">66</a>] summarized that oxygen-containing groups of GO were generally decomposed in three temperature regions of 170–250, 500–600 and 750–1000 °C. In our case, i.e., the composite with incorporated GO, the decomposition in the middle region mentioned in the literature is hardly detectable.</p>
<p>It should also be noted that in the case of the samples after HTT (see <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>B,D and <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Section S3.2.2 in the Supplementary Materials</a>), the signal intensities were much lower and almost no changes between ~150–300 °C were observed. Obviously, as a result of the high-temperature heat treatment, only moisture desorption was present in these cases. In the case of the parent GNP, the signal intensity is the weakest in all regions, proving that there are only a very limited number of functional groups on GNPs (line e in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>).</p>
<p>All these observations indicate that not only the mixed oxide part of the composite, but also its carbonaceous content, undergo significant changes during HTT. In good agreement with the TG-MS investigations, XPS measurements also confirmed removal of a significant amount of the functional groups of GO, resulting in similar, graphite-like C 1s spectra for all investigated composites.</p>
<p>SEM images provided information about the morphology of the surface of the composites with different GNPs/GO ratios (see <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>). The texture of the composite gradually changed with increasing GO content. Layer-like structures attributed to the GO-derived material became more dominant from composite to composite, alongside irregular rectangles derived from GNPs.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-f003"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12693164_nanomaterials-15-01753-g003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/029a/12693164/eaf4ae947389/nanomaterials-15-01753-g003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="992" width="680" alt="Figure 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/nanomaterials-15-01753-f003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>SEM images of Ti<sub>(1−x)</sub>Mo<sub>x</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C composites prepared from GNP-GO mixtures with different GNPs/GO ratios. (<strong>A</strong>,<strong>B</strong>) 100GNP-S2, (<strong>C</strong>,<strong>D</strong>) 75GNP-S2, (<strong>E</strong>,<strong>F</strong>) 50GNP-S2, (<strong>G</strong>,<strong>H</strong>) 25GNP-S2. Column I: 12,000× magnification; Column II: 200,000× magnification.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec3dot2dot2-nanomaterials-15-01753"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2. Characterization of Composite-Supported Platinum Catalyst</h4>
<p>According to XRD measurement (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>C), all the composite-supported catalysts contained Pt in highly dispersed form. A broad band at about 40° (Pt(111) reflection) appeared (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>C) in all samples. Average crystallite size values of Pt calculated from these patterns were relatively similar (see <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>).</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/nanomaterials-15-01753-t004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn5"><p><sup>1</sup> Average crystallite size of Pt calculated from Scherrer equation; <sup>2</sup> Calculated without the Pt content.</p></div></div></section><p>This result can be attributed to the EG-assisted NaBH<sub>4</sub> reduction (see details in <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Section S1.2 in the Supplementary Materials</a>), which was found to be a very effective Pt loading method in our previous works [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>]. The Pt content of the various samples measured by ICP-OES, a bulk technique, was only slightly less than the nominal value (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>). The same can be observed in the case of the introduced Mo dopant. In contrast, XPS revealed a very high apparent Pt content for all samples; this can be explained by the localization of the Pt particles on the surface of relatively large support grains. A notable feature is that the apparent oxide content derived from XPS measurements is smaller in all samples than the nominal value. In fact, this phenomenon was generally observed for mixed oxide–carbon composite materials [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>] and was explained by the existence of large oxide crystals: if a part of the oxide material is present in the form of particles thicker than the information depth of XPS (around 10 nm), its amount is underestimated, as the oxide material in the interior of these particles is not accessible for the method. At the same time, the surface Mo content was slightly higher (i.e., smaller Ti/Mo value) than that observed by ICP-OES, but the sample-to-sample differences were small (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>).</p>
<p>The XPS results concerning the chemical states of the components of the electrocatalyst samples are detailed in <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Section S3.2.3 in the Supplementary Materials</a> (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Table S7</a>, <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figures S9 and S10</a>). In fact, many characteristics such as the completely oxidized nature of Ti and the coexistence of the dominant Mo<sup>6+</sup> states with the less abundant Mo<sup>5+</sup> and Mo<sup>4+</sup> states in the mixed oxide, the metallic nature of Pt or the predominantly graphitic state of the carbonaceous component were very similar to those documented in other mixed oxide–carbon composite-supported systems [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>,<a href="#B18-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-nanomaterials-15-01753">18</a>]. C 1s spectra (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S10</a>) demonstrated the transformation of the GO component into a predominantly graphitic material during the catalyst synthesis procedure: its functional groups containing carbon singly bound to oxygen were largely eliminated or (in a small fraction) further oxidized into a carboxyl/lactone-like state [<a href="#B67-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67-nanomaterials-15-01753">67</a>]. Nevertheless, both the C 1s and O 1s spectra revealed that the amount of these types of oxygen-containing functional groups slightly increased with higher nominal GO content. As no correlation was observed by TG-MS between the functional group content of the composites and their GO content after HTT, this kind of functionalization can be regarded as a surface phenomenon.</p>
<p>TEM micrographs of the electrocatalyst samples are shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a> and <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S11</a>. At low magnification (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S11A,C,E</a>) the objects providing dark contrast are large mixed oxide crystals, which frequently formed flower-like aggregates and often covered the major part of the catalyst grains. In case of the Pt/100GNP-S2 electrocatalyst, individual catalyst grains were scattered through the field of view (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S11A</a>). If the support contained GO, sheets, sometimes with wrinkled paper-like appearance, were frequently attached to the grains carrying the large oxide crystals; these sheets are identified as the derivatives of GO formed during the synthesis of the composite support (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S11C,E</a>). In the case of the Pt/50GNP-S2 sample (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S11C</a>), the sheets formed interconnections between the grains with large oxide crystals. In the case of the Pt/25GNP-S2 catalyst (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>J,K,L and <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S11E</a>), the sheet-like objects represented the major part of the backbone of the support. Micrographs taken at higher magnification (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a> and <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S11B,D,F</a>) confirm that Pt existed in a highly dispersed form of particles, which in places formed some small aggregates, while the mixed oxide appeared both in the form of small and large crystals in all samples. Smaller oxide crystals with low contrast were typically found in the sheet-like regions (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S11D,F</a>). At high resolution, apart from the abundant fringes of the Pt nanoparticles, occasionally fringes separated by some 0.342–0.344 nm were also visible, clearly demonstrating the presence of multilayered carbon particles (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S11B,G</a>). The more ordered carbon particles (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S11B</a>) can be identified as GNPs or their fragments formed during composite synthesis. Since these features were observable in all samples, it has to be assumed that the support grains always contained a considerable amount of stacked GNPs and/or GNP fragments. The less ordered ones may also be regarded as agglomerated sheets of GO reduced by HTT.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-f004"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12693164_nanomaterials-15-01753-g004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/029a/12693164/67b1b751c334/nanomaterials-15-01753-g004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="897" width="678" alt="Figure 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/nanomaterials-15-01753-f004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>TEM images of Pt/Ti<sub>(1−x)</sub>Mo<sub>x</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C catalysts prepared from GNP-GO mixtures with different GNPs/GO ratios. (<strong>A</strong>–<strong>C</strong>) Pt/100GNP-S2, (<strong>D</strong>–<strong>F</strong>) Pt/75GNP-S2, (<strong>G</strong>–<strong>I</strong>) Pt/50GNP-S2, (<strong>J</strong>–<strong>L</strong>) Pt/25GNP-S2.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Element maps of Pt/100GNP-S2 (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>) were almost completely congruent for Ti, O and Mo which can be proof that Mo incorporated and/or tightly bound to the mixed oxide surface, as we found previously [<a href="#B18-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-nanomaterials-15-01753">18</a>]. It is conceivable that the formation of the bent shapes seen in the O, Ti and Mo element maps was induced by the shape of the GNP particles (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>C–E). The elemental map of Pt was less strongly correlated with those of Mo and Ti, indicating slightly more spreading of the active metal on the surface (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>F). A similar phenomenon can be observed in the element map of the Pt/25GNP-S2 sample (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>C–E). The Ti, O and Mo maps of the Pt/25GNP-S2 sample also clearly show the existence of curved shapes. The possibility cannot be excluded that these objects originated from the mixed oxide formed at the edges of the GNP. However, the Pt map did not follow the aforementioned pattern. The curved shapes are also found in the element maps of samples Pt/75GNP-S2 and Pt/50GNP-S2 (see <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figures S12 and S13</a> in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-f005"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12693164_nanomaterials-15-01753-g005.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/029a/12693164/93d23eb63eb6/nanomaterials-15-01753-g005.jpg" loading="lazy" height="498" width="748" alt="Figure 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/nanomaterials-15-01753-f005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Elemental map of Pt/100GNP-S2 electrocatalyst. (<strong>A</strong>) HAADF, (<strong>B</strong>) C, (<strong>C</strong>) Ti, (<strong>D</strong>) Mo, (<strong>E</strong>) O, (<strong>F</strong>): Pt.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-f006"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12693164_nanomaterials-15-01753-g006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/029a/12693164/2f971d282c24/nanomaterials-15-01753-g006.jpg" loading="lazy" height="492" width="748" alt="Figure 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/nanomaterials-15-01753-f006/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Elemental map of Pt/25GNP-S2 electrocatalyst. (<strong>A</strong>) HAADF, (<strong>B</strong>) C, (<strong>C</strong>) Ti, (<strong>D</strong>) Mo, (<strong>E</strong>) O, (<strong>F</strong>) Pt.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The electrical conductivity of all composite-supported catalysts was much lower than that of the Pt/C catalysts (see <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>). This is not a surprise, because only a quarter of the weight of the support was carbon, and the rest was built from a TiO<sub>2</sub>-based mixed oxide, which can be considered a semiconductor. The conductivity of samples containing the GNPs-GO mixture-derived carbon decreased with decreasing GNPs content, which can be explained by the excellent conductivity of the starting GNPs, while GO is a worse conductor even in its graphitized form.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-f007"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 7.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12693164_nanomaterials-15-01753-g007.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/029a/12693164/d78ca8d6f37c/nanomaterials-15-01753-g007.jpg" loading="lazy" height="657" width="727" alt="Figure 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/nanomaterials-15-01753-f007/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Electrical conductivities of the catalysts. Pt/C: commercial reference catalyst (Quintech).</p></figcaption></figure><p>Surprisingly, the pure GNPs-originated catalyst had the lowest electrical conductivity in these series. A possible explanation is that the carbonaceous aggregates of the support, carrying and/or intermixed with the oxide, are separated by the (frequently large) surface oxide crystals (<a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S11A</a>), as the formation of TiO<sub>2</sub> starts at the functional groups located at the edges of the little GNP units and spreads from there.</p>
<p>However, if there is a close interaction between the larger GO sheets and GNP units, as seen in the TEM image published in the literature (Figure 4 in Ref. [<a href="#B48-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-nanomaterials-15-01753">48</a>]) and as depicted in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-sch001" class="usa-link">Scheme 1</a>, or GO bridges are formed between the GNP nanoparticles or their assemblies in the starting carbonaceous material, a percolating carbon network is formed during titania formation in the mixed oxide–carbon composite, which contributes to the increased conductivity. We believe such a situation is shown in <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S11C</a>. It is important to mention that the intense ultrasonic treatment, which is necessary to apply during aqueous synthesis due to the strongly apolar nature of GNPs, is able to rupture the GO sheets and reduce the extension of the carbon networks.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-sch001"><h5 class="obj_head">Scheme 1.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12693164_nanomaterials-15-01753-sch001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/029a/12693164/2493865473c7/nanomaterials-15-01753-sch001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="480" width="672" alt="Scheme 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/nanomaterials-15-01753-sch001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Schematic view of the sticking of the GNP particles to the surface of the delaminated GO sheet (<strong>left</strong>) and formation of the oxide particles around the GNP particles and on the GO sheet at the beginning (<strong>right</strong>). According to the results described in this and previous works [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>], some of the oxide particles develop into large rod-like structures (represented by black sticks), which may form flower-like aggregates, while other oxide particles remain small and give minimal contrast in TEM, although their presence is revealed by strong Pt-oxide coupling in electrochemical experiments (light-gray oxide elements).</p></figcaption></figure><p>The increasing presence of the GO derivatives with increasing nominal GO content was already suggested by the SEM results of the composite support (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>). If one assumes that the increasing GO content results in better interconnections, while GO is a worse conductor than GNPs, even the optimum observed in the conductivity–GO content relationship (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>) can be understood.</p>
<p>In summary, based on the physicochemical characterization of the catalysts, it can be concluded that the composite-supported catalyst samples prepared with and without GO showed similar oxide structures and similarly high dispersion and homogeneous distribution of Pt. However, their texture and electrical conductivity clearly reflected the presence of GO in the system.</p></section></section><section id="sec3dot3-nanomaterials-15-01753"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3. Electrochemical Behavior of Pt/Ti<sub>(1−x)</sub>Mo<sub>x</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C Composite Type of Catalysts Derived from GNPs-GO Mixtures with Different GNPs/GO Ratios</h3>
<p>The effect of the GNPs/GO ratio in the mixture of two carbonaceous components used for the preparation of the Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C composite-supported 20 wt.% Pt catalysts on the electrochemical performance was investigated.</p>
<p>In addition, the results obtained on the reference Pt/C and catalysts containing only GNPs (Pt/100GNP-S2) or GO (Pt/100GO) as the starting carbonaceous materials are also presented.</p>
<p>The cyclic voltammograms of the electrocatalysts recorded in 0.5 M H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> before and after 500 polarization cycles of the stability test are demonstrated in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>; for comparison, <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>A also shows the CVs of the reference Pt/C catalyst. As shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>, the voltammograms of all Mo-containing catalysts exhibited the usual redox peak pair at 380–530 mV in addition to the typical under-potentially deposited hydrogen adsorption/desorption between 50 and 350 mV characteristic of Pt catalysts (see CV of the reference Pt/C).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-f008"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12693164_nanomaterials-15-01753-g008.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/029a/12693164/dad71af3d7dc/nanomaterials-15-01753-g008.jpg" loading="lazy" height="228" width="733" alt="Figure 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/nanomaterials-15-01753-f008/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Cyclic voltammograms of the electrocatalysts recorded in 0.5 M H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> before (solid curves) and after 500 cycles (dashed curves) of the stability test: (<strong>A</strong>) Pt/100GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>), Pt/50GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>), Pt/100GO (<em>█</em>) and reference Pt/C (<em>█</em>); (<strong>B</strong>) Pt/75GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>), Pt/50GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>) and Pt/25GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>). Sweep rate: 100 mV/s.</p></figcaption></figure><p>As shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>, the voltammograms of all Mo-containing catalysts exhibited the usual redox peak pair at 380–530 mV in addition to the typical under-potentially deposited hydrogen adsorption/desorption between 50 and 350 mV characteristic of Pt catalysts (see CV of the reference Pt/C). The presence of these Mo redox peaks confirms the statement in the literature [<a href="#B68-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68-nanomaterials-15-01753">68</a>,<a href="#B69-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B69-nanomaterials-15-01753">69</a>,<a href="#B70-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B70-nanomaterials-15-01753">70</a>] that an active interface exists between the Pt nanoparticles and the Mo-containing support, as already demonstrated in our earlier studies [<a href="#B61-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-nanomaterials-15-01753">61</a>,<a href="#B71-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B71-nanomaterials-15-01753">71</a>].</p>
<p>According to the extensive literature on this subject [<a href="#B68-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68-nanomaterials-15-01753">68</a>,<a href="#B70-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B70-nanomaterials-15-01753">70</a>], the redox peak pair observed in Mo-containing Pt catalytic systems is generally considered to be the intercalation/de-intercalation of H atoms into the MoO<sub>x</sub> lattice leading to the formation/decomposition of hydrogen molybdenum bronze species and, as a consequence, should be subtracted from the calculated H<sub>UPD</sub> charges.</p>
<p>The electrochemically active Pt surface area of the catalysts determined for all catalysts in the first cycle (see ECSA<sub>1</sub> values presented in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>) was calculated from the oxidation charge of the monolayer hydrogen [<a href="#B72-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B72-nanomaterials-15-01753">72</a>], taking into account the capacitive currents, originated from the double-layer charging of the CVs [<a href="#B73-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73-nanomaterials-15-01753">73</a>,<a href="#B74-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B74-nanomaterials-15-01753">74</a>] and considering the above-mentioned overlapping features of different origin in the voltammograms of the Mo-containing catalysts, which may lead to some uncertainty in the results obtained.</p>
