<td align="left" valign="middle" style="border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Pt/C <sup>6</sup>
</td>
<td align="center" valign="middle" style="border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">805</td>
<td align="center" valign="middle" style="border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">87.2 ± 2.3</td>
<td align="center" valign="middle" style="border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">12.7</td>
<td align="center" valign="middle" style="border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">47.8</td>
<td align="center" valign="middle" style="border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">33.2</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/nanomaterials-15-01753-t005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn6"><p><sup>1</sup> The position of the main CO stripping peak measured on fresh catalysts; <sup>2</sup> The average ECSA<sub>1</sub> value obtained on fresh catalysts; <sup>3</sup> ΔECSA<sub>500</sub> and ΔECSA<sub>10,000</sub> were calculated from the charges originated from the hydrogen desorption in the 1st and 500th or 10,000th cycles according to Equation (S2) in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials (Section S2.2)</a>; <sup>4</sup> ΔECSA<sub>500</sub> values were calculated from the 10,000-cycle stability measurements; <sup>5</sup> Mass activity (MA) at 0.9 V obtained in the ORR experiments on an RDE at 1600 rp; <sup>6</sup> From Ref. [<a href="#B75-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B75-nanomaterials-15-01753">75</a>]; <em>s</em>h = should<em>er</em>.</p></div></div></section><p>As can be seen from <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>, the presence of Mo redox peaks of different sizes prevents the use of a consistent potential range for calculating the ECSA values using the H<sub>UPD</sub> method. By slightly modifying the potential ranges used for H<sub>UPD</sub> integration for the Mo-containing catalysts presented in this work, it was possible to perform the calculation in such a way that the Mo oxidation peak was not included. This may result in slightly lower ECSA values compared to catalysts where such peaks are absent, as in the case of the reference Pt/C catalyst, or where these redox peaks are not as pronounced. However, it should be emphasized that in the case of Mo-containing catalysts, these overlapping peaks can be separated much more easily compared to, for example, the Pt/WO<sub>x</sub> type of electrocatalysts, in which the peak observed in the negative-going scan at 200 mV vs. RHE, attributed to the formation of tungsten bronzes H<sub>y</sub>WO<sub>3</sub> or substoichiometric tungsten oxides WO<sub>3-x</sub> [<a href="#B76-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B76-nanomaterials-15-01753">76</a>], excludes the possibility of determining the ECSA using the H<sub>UPD</sub> method.</p>
<p>It should be noted that, as shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>, in this series of experiments there was a fairly large difference in the ECSA<sub>1</sub> values obtained for the different catalysts prepared using GNPs-GO mixture with various GNPs/GO ratios. According to the ECSA<sub>1</sub> values shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>, Pt/75GNP-S2 has the lowest electrochemically active Pt surface area, which can be related the lowest Pt content (16.5 wt.%), determined in this series of experiments by ICP-OES (see <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>). In addition to the slightly lower Pt loading, the markedly lower ECSA<sub>1</sub> observed for Pt/75GNP-S2 can be attributed to the microstructural characteristics of the support at this specific GNPs/GO ratio. Although this sample exhibits a relatively high BET surface area, its lower total pore volume and potentially less favorable pore connectivity likely limit electrolyte access to a portion of the Pt surface. In contrast, the Pt/50GNP-S2 catalyst, which contains a more balanced distribution of GO anchoring sites and GNP conductive domains with a more open composite structure, facilitates uniform Pt dispersion and improved electrochemical accessibility. This indicates that the relationship between GNPs/GO ratio and ECSA is not linear, and that optimal Pt utilization is achieved when sufficient GO is present to provide enough functional groups for anchoring nanoparticles [<a href="#B77-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B77-nanomaterials-15-01753">77</a>], while GNP content remains high enough to maintain electronic conductivity and structural openness for appropriate mass transport. Since Pt/50GNP-S2 has both higher intrinsic surface (slightly higher loading) and a more favorable support microstructure, the absolute number of electrochemically accessible Pt sites is much larger than in Pt/75GNP-S2, resulting in higher ECSA<sub>1</sub>.</p>
<p>As shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>, only relatively small changes in the voltammogram shape after the 500 polarization cycles of the stability tests can be seen on the catalysts containing pure GNPs (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>A) and GNPs-GO mixtures (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>B). As demonstrated in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>A, the most pronounced change was observed on the Pt/100GO catalyst and the reference Pt/C.</p>
<p><a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a> summarizes the values of the ECSA loss observed after the 500 and 10,000 polarization cycles of the stability tests (ΔECSA<sub>500</sub> and ΔECSA<sub>10,000</sub>, respectively) between 50 and 1000 mV potential limits. For convenience, the ΔECSA<sub>500</sub> values presented in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a> were calculated from data collected during stability measurements over 10,000 polarization cycles. As already mentioned above, the most pronounced changes were observed on the Pt/100GO catalyst (see <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>A), which was reflected in the highest ΔECSA<sub>500</sub> value obtained on the Mo-containing catalysts presented in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>. But an even higher ΔECSA<sub>500</sub> value was obtained on the commercial Pt/C catalyst (ΔECSA<sub>500</sub> = 12.7%).</p>
<p>However, in this regard, a more informative comparison of the stability of the catalysts studied can be obtained based on the results of the long-term stability tests. The changes in the shape of the cyclic voltammograms obtained during the 10,000 polarization cycles are demonstrated in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a> and <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S17</a> in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a>. <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a> compares the results obtained on the reference Pt/C and the Pt/50GNP-S2 sample, while the cyclic voltammograms of other composite-supported catalysts are presented in <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S17</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-f009"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 9.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12693164_nanomaterials-15-01753-g009.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/029a/12693164/e3bdb3e19158/nanomaterials-15-01753-g009.jpg" loading="lazy" height="210" width="755" alt="Figure 9"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/nanomaterials-15-01753-f009/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Cyclic voltammograms of the reference Pt/C (<strong>A</strong>) and Pt/50GNP-S2 (<strong>B</strong>) electrocatalysts obtained during a 10,000-cycle stability test. Recorded in 0.5 M H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> solution with 100 mV s<sup>−1</sup> sweep rate; T = 25 °C.</p></figcaption></figure><p>As expected, during the long-term stability test, the main changes in the shape of the voltammograms are associated with the hydrogen adsorption/desorption peaks between 50 and 350 mV and the Pt oxidation/reduction peaks above 800 mV. As shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a>, the difference in the behavior of the commercial Pt/C and the most stable Pt/50GNP-S2 electrocatalyst during polarization for 10,000 cycles becomes obvious after 2500 cycles: while on the Pt/50GNP-S2 catalyst the obtained cyclic voltammograms of the 2500, 5000 and 10,000 cycles are practically indistinguishable (see <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a>B), the reference Pt/C demonstrates much more significant changes (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a>A).</p>
<p>The changes in the ECSA over 10,000 CV cycles for the reference Pt/C and Mo-containing Pt electrocatalysts are presented in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f010" class="usa-link">Figure 10</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-f010"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 10.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12693164_nanomaterials-15-01753-g010.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/029a/12693164/d8417d18f9b5/nanomaterials-15-01753-g010.jpg" loading="lazy" height="395" width="749" alt="Figure 10"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/nanomaterials-15-01753-f010/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Change in the ECSA during 10,000 polarization cycles for the reference Pt/C (<em>█</em>) and composite-supported electrocatalysts: Pt/100GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>), Pt/75GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>), Pt/50GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>), Pt/25GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>) and Pt/100GO (<em>█</em>). ECSA<sub>N</sub>/ECSA<sub>1</sub> values are compared as a function of the cycles number.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Similar behavior in the long-term stability tests was recently demonstrated for Sn-containing composite-supported electrocatalysts [<a href="#B78-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B78-nanomaterials-15-01753">78</a>]. For the Pt/Ti<sub>0.9</sub>Sn<sub>0.1</sub>O<sub>2</sub>–C catalytic systems, it was noticed that the initial period (up to 2500 polarization cycles), characterized by a pronounced decrease in the normalized ECSA values, was followed by a period of a very moderate decrease in the ECSA<sub>N</sub>/ECSA<sub>1</sub> values. This behavior is confirmed in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f010" class="usa-link">Figure 10</a> for the Pt/50GNP-S2 electrocatalyst, where the observed changes on this catalyst after 2500 cycles are very small. As shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f010" class="usa-link">Figure 10</a>, a similar behavior to the Pt/50GNP-S2 catalyst is also observed for the Pt/100GO sample, showing a significant slowdown in the decrease rate in the normalized ECSA value after 2500 polarization cycles. However, as can be seen from <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f010" class="usa-link">Figure 10</a>, for the other three GNP-containing catalysts, a similar pronounced slowdown in the decline of the normalized values of the ECSA is observed only after 5000 cycles of cyclic polarization. Meanwhile, for Pt/C, a constant decrease in the ECSA value is characteristic throughout the 10,000-cycle stability test. Thus, taking into account the tendency of a sharper drop in the electrochemical surface area of Pt in the reference Pt/C catalyst, it can be assumed that when conducting longer experiments, the difference in the stability of the reference and composite-supported Pt electrocatalysts will be even more pronounced.</p>
<p><a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f010" class="usa-link">Figure 10</a> also presents the results obtained from the 500-cycle stability test, which demonstrate some small increase in ECSA during the short-term stability tests for two catalysts (Pt/50GNP-S2 and Pt/25GNP-S2), reflected by the negative values for ΔECSA<sub>500</sub> presented in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>. This behavior may be due to the cleaning of the catalyst surface from remaining contaminants or oxides that have a blocking effect [<a href="#B18-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-nanomaterials-15-01753">18</a>,<a href="#B75-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B75-nanomaterials-15-01753">75</a>].</p>
<p>Based on our synthesis route (for details see <a href="#sec2dot2-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Section 2.2</a>), the most probable blocking species that are electrochemically removed during the first cycles are (i) organic-origin residues (adsorbed ethylene glycol decomposition products, incomplete reduction by-products or remaining solvent fragments), (ii) adsorbed anions (residual Cl<sup>−</sup> from Pt precursor and from the HCl treatment) that can block adsorption sites and (iii) native Pt oxides/hydroxides (PtO<sub>x</sub>) formed on the metal surface during handling and drying. In addition, oxygen-containing functional groups on GO (–OH, –COOH, epoxide) and impurities in the parent GO suspension can both partially block Pt sites or alter Pt-support interaction and change during electrochemical conditioning. It is important to note that our protocol includes four centrifugal washing cycles to remove chloride; nevertheless, trace Cl<sup>−</sup> cannot be fully excluded. In addition, in our previous study it has been demonstrated [<a href="#B75-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B75-nanomaterials-15-01753">75</a>] that a similar effect is observed during reductive pretreatment of Mo-containing Pt electrocatalysts, which leads to the migration of Mo onto the surface of Pt particles and the blocking of this surface. During the first cycles of the polarization, removal of these species and releasing the Pt particle surface may lead to a temporary increase in the ECSA values.</p>
<p>In our previous studies, we found that carbon materials behave as a solid template during the synthesis of composite materials using our sol–gel method. The key to high stability is obtaining the homogeneous distribution of mixed oxide layers or particles over a carbon backbone [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>,<a href="#B30-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-nanomaterials-15-01753">30</a>]. The best results on electrocatalysts with 20 wt.% Pt were obtained using composite supports with increased carbon content (50–75 wt.%) and/or upon using functionalized carbon materials [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>,<a href="#B30-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-nanomaterials-15-01753">30</a>]. We propose that carbon functionalization alters to some extent the nucleation and growth of the mixed oxide particles over the carbon surface, resulting in a more uniform distribution of the mixed oxide.</p>
<p>As can be seen from <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f010" class="usa-link">Figure 10</a> and <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>, the most pronounced ECSA decrease was observed on the reference Pt/C (ΔECSA<sub>10,000</sub> = 47.8%). The best stability after 10,000 polarization cycles was demonstrated by the Pt/50GNP-S2 catalyst with a GNPs/GO = 50/50 ratio, which is reflected in the lowest values of ΔECSA<sub>10,000</sub>, presented in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a> (ΔECSA<sub>10,000</sub> = 21.2%). The other two catalysts, prepared using the GNPs-GO mixture with different GNPs/GO ratios, have slightly higher ΔECSA values (31 and 33%; cf. ΔECSA<sub>10,000</sub> values for the Pt/25GNP-S2 and Pt/75GNP-S2 catalysts in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>). However, it should be noted that catalysts prepared using only GNPs (Pt/100GNP-S2) or GO (Pt/100GO) as starting carbonaceous materials also exhibit good stability (~36%), characteristic of Mo-containing systems with high mixed oxide content (Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>/C ratios of 75/25 [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>]).</p>
<p>Our best results from the 10,000-cycle stability tests obtained on 20 wt.% Pt/Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C catalysts prepared using different carbonaceous material and various post-treatments are summarized in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t006" class="usa-link">Table 6</a>.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-t006"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 6.</h4>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/nanomaterials-15-01753-t006/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn7"><p><sup>1</sup> BP: Black Pearls 2000; <sup>2</sup> Reduction in H<sub>2</sub> flow for 2 h (to induce the SMSI effect); <sup>3</sup> ST: solvothermal treatment.</p></div></div></section><p>According to the results presented in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t006" class="usa-link">Table 6</a>, the combination of various unmodified, functionalized and non-traditional carbon materials with different treatments carried out during or after sol–gel synthesis resulted in innovative supported Pt electrocatalysts with excellent long-term stability. Thus, as shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t006" class="usa-link">Table 6</a>, the results for the Pt/50GNP-S2 catalyst, containing GNPs-GO mixture with the ratio of 50/50, are among the best ones we have obtained on Mo-containing composite-supported Pt catalysts.</p>
<p>High CO electrooxidation activity is critical to maintaining the performance and longevity of fuel cells, since the presence of even small traces of CO in hydrogen gas leads to the poisoning of catalysts and deteriorates their activity [<a href="#B79-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79-nanomaterials-15-01753">79</a>,<a href="#B80-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B80-nanomaterials-15-01753">80</a>]. The traditional Pt/C catalysts are able to oxidize CO only at about 800 mV [<a href="#B81-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B81-nanomaterials-15-01753">81</a>,<a href="#B82-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B82-nanomaterials-15-01753">82</a>]. However, under practical fuel cell operating conditions, such a high potential is never reached at the anode. In this regard, the key factor determining CO tolerance is the ability of electrocatalysts to oxidize CO at low potentials (below 600 mV). From this point of view, a state-of-the-art PtRu/C catalyst may be a good choice. However, the instability of Ru increases significantly at anode potentials ~450 mV vs. RHE, leading to the dissolution of ruthenium and degradation of the catalyst [<a href="#B83-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B83-nanomaterials-15-01753">83</a>].</p>
<p>It is well known in the literature [<a href="#B80-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B80-nanomaterials-15-01753">80</a>] and our studies [<a href="#B58-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-nanomaterials-15-01753">58</a>] that, due to the ability of molybdenum to adsorb hydroxyl groups via a bifunctional mechanism, catalysts containing Mo have increased resistance to CO poisoning and can oxidize CO even at very low electrode potentials. As has been repeatedly noted in our previous studies [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>,<a href="#B61-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-nanomaterials-15-01753">61</a>], the most important indicator of the CO-tolerant behavior of these catalytic systems is the increase in current in the “pre-peak” region (observed between 50 and 500 mV), which precedes the main peak of CO electrooxidation at higher potential. In this regard, our previous results have demonstrated the increased CO tolerance and better electrocatalytic stability of the Pt/Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C electrocatalyst compared to the PtRu/C [<a href="#B57-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-nanomaterials-15-01753">57</a>]. We demonstrated that CO electrooxidation began on the Mo-containing catalysts at 50 mV, while on the PtRu/C the E<sub>CO,onset</sub> was observed at 255 mV.</p>
<p>It should be noted that the “pre-peak” region is complex and includes contributions from the oxidation of both weakly bound CO on Pt in close proximity to Mo, which provides the OH<sub>ads</sub> required for the reaction at low potential, and the oxidation of Mo surface species [<a href="#B61-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-nanomaterials-15-01753">61</a>]. On the other hand, it can be argued that the presence of a “pre-peak” region on the CO<sub>ads</sub>-stripping voltammogram demonstrates the close location of Pt and easily reducible Mo species [<a href="#B30-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-nanomaterials-15-01753">30</a>,<a href="#B84-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B84-nanomaterials-15-01753">84</a>,<a href="#B85-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B85-nanomaterials-15-01753">85</a>].</p>
<p>Considering the above-mentioned facts, it is important to emphasize that there is no relationship between the magnitude of the main peak of CO electrooxidation, which in the case of the reference Pt/C catalyst was observed at around 805 mV (see <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>), and the activity in this reaction.</p>
<p>The activity of the prepared Mo-containing Pt catalysts and the reference Pt/C in the CO electrooxidation was studied using CO<sub>ads</sub>-stripping voltammetry measurements, and the results obtained are presented in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f011" class="usa-link">Figure 11</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-f011"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 11.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12693164_nanomaterials-15-01753-g011.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/029a/12693164/11262e498fb3/nanomaterials-15-01753-g011.jpg" loading="lazy" height="223" width="736" alt="Figure 11"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/nanomaterials-15-01753-f011/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>CO<sub>ads</sub>-stripping voltammograms of the electrocatalysts recorded in 0.5 M H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub>: (<strong>A</strong>) Pt/100GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>), Pt/50GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>) and Pt/100GO (<em>█</em>); (<strong>B</strong>) Pt/75GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>), Pt/50GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>) and Pt/25GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>). Results obtained on the reference Pt/C (<em>█</em>) were included for comparison. Sweep rate: 10 mV/s.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The activity in this reaction is expressed by both the initial potential (E<sub>CO,onset</sub>) and the position of the main peak (E<sub>CO,max</sub>) of CO oxidation [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>,<a href="#B61-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-nanomaterials-15-01753">61</a>]. As can be seen from <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f011" class="usa-link">Figure 11</a>, for all Mo-containing composite-supported catalysts presented in this study, the E<sub>CO,onset</sub> of the CO oxidation did not exceed 50–100 mV. The low E<sub>CO,onset</sub> and the presence of a “pre-peak” are evidence that in a PEM fuel cell, at the operating anode voltage, the electrode containing this catalyst is capable of oxidizing CO.</p>
<p>Confirmation of this was obtained in previously conducted fuel cell experiments carried out using a CO contaminant containing H<sub>2</sub> [<a href="#B86-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B86-nanomaterials-15-01753">86</a>] and reformate gas [<a href="#B87-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B87-nanomaterials-15-01753">87</a>].</p>
<p>As shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f011" class="usa-link">Figure 11</a>, a characteristic “pre-peak” is observed on all CO<sub>ads</sub>-stripping voltammograms of Mo-containing catalysts; the most pronounced “pre-peak” area was observed on the Pt/100GO catalyst (see <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f011" class="usa-link">Figure 11</a>A). In our recent study [<a href="#B17-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-nanomaterials-15-01753">17</a>], this behavior was explained by the presence of a fairly large number of very small Pt nanoparticles (less than 1 nm) in this sample.</p>
<p>In addition, it should be noted that for the studied Mo-containing catalysts, the ECSA values determined from CO stripping are systematically lower than those derived from H<sub>UPD</sub> (by approx. 27–37% for the synthesized GO-containing electrocatalysts and ~9% for Pt/100GNP sample). Considering this fact, and the presence of a CO pre-oxidation feature, which appears at significantly lower potentials than the main CO oxidation peak, the use of the main CO oxidation peak alone for ECSA determination can underestimate the true ECSA values for these materials. This is why we rely on H<sub>UPD</sub>-derived ECSA values when comparing intrinsic activity.</p>
<p>The values of the position of the main CO stripping peak maximum (E<sub>CO,max</sub>), observed on the CO<sub>ads</sub>-stripping curves on these electrocatalysts, are presented in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a>. As follows from <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a> and <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f011" class="usa-link">Figure 11</a>, all GO-containing catalysts showed a CO<sub>ads</sub>-electrooxidation peak at about 705 mV overlapping with a shoulder at 745 mV. On the pure GNP-containing composite-supported catalyst (Pt/100GNP), the broad CO<sub>ads</sub>-stripping peak was located at ca. 765 mV, while in the case of the reference Pt/C catalyst, the corresponding peak was at about 805 mV (see <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a> and <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f011" class="usa-link">Figure 11</a>). According to these results, it can be concluded that the presence of GO in composite materials leads to an increase in the tolerance of catalysts to CO.</p>
<p>In conclusion, enhanced CO tolerance of the Pt/Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C catalytic systems was evidenced by the appearance of a CO-oxidation-related “pre-peak” and by a considerable shift in the maximum of the main CO oxidation peak towards less positive potential compared to the reference commercial Pt/C.</p>
<p>Given their high CO tolerance, these catalysts are generally recommended for use on the anode side of fuel cells, but taking into account the existence of the close contact between Pt and Mo, it is also worth investigating their properties in the ORR. As already mentioned in <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Section S2.2 of the Supplementary Materials</a>, the activity of these catalysts in the ORR was studied by the RDE technique in 0.5 M H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> solution saturated with O<sub>2</sub> at six rotation speeds (225, 400, 625, 900, 1225 and 1600 rpm). Increasing the electrode rotation speed (see <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S14</a> in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a>), as expected, resulted in an increase in the current density (<em>j</em>) in the potential dynamic polarization curves, indicating faster diffusion of oxygen to the catalyst surface. <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f012" class="usa-link">Figure 12</a> compares the ORR catalytic activity of the commercial Pt/C and Mo-containing Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>–C composite-supported Pt electrocatalysts at 1600 rpm. Using the same rotation speed allows a direct and fair comparison between the reference Pt/C and the newly synthesized catalyst under identical hydrodynamic conditions.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="nanomaterials-15-01753-f012"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 12.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12693164_nanomaterials-15-01753-g012.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/029a/12693164/4ba04a3eb81a/nanomaterials-15-01753-g012.jpg" loading="lazy" height="286" width="747" alt="Figure 12"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/nanomaterials-15-01753-f012/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Electrochemical characterization in the ORR obtained before (solid lines) and after 500 cycles (dotted lines) of the stability test presented as current density in mA/cm<sup>2</sup> (<strong>A</strong>) and mass activity in mA/μg<sub>Pt</sub> (<strong>B</strong>) of the reference Pt/C (<em>█</em>) and Pt/Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C electrocatalysts: Pt/100GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>), Pt/75GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>), Pt/50GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>), Pt/25GNP-S2 (<em>█</em>) and Pt/100GO (<em>█</em>). <em>j</em> vs. <em>E</em> curves were recorded in O<sub>2</sub>-saturated 0.5 M H<sub>2</sub>SO<sub>4</sub> on an RDE at 1600 rpm. Sweep rate: 10 mV/s. The catalysts’ Pt loading is included in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>.</p></figcaption></figure><p>First of all, it should be emphasized that the interpretation of the results of RDE measurements obtained on powder electrocatalysts with a high SSA should be carried out with caution [<a href="#B88-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B88-nanomaterials-15-01753">88</a>,<a href="#B89-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B89-nanomaterials-15-01753">89</a>]. However, in the case of studying catalysts with similar composition and structure, using the same method of electrode preparation, the RDE results can facilitate their qualitative comparison.</p>
<p>Very close values of the onset potentials for the ORR (E<sub>ORR,onset</sub>~955 ± 10 mV) were observed for all catalysts studied, indicating good activity in this reaction. The electrocatalytic efficiency of the catalysts in the ORR was presented as values normalized with respect to the geometric area of the electrode (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f012" class="usa-link">Figure 12</a>A), and as mass activity (MA) in units of mA/μg<sub>Pt</sub> (<a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f012" class="usa-link">Figure 12</a>B).</p>
<p>The ORR current density in the mixed kinetic–diffusion controlled region presented in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f012" class="usa-link">Figure 12</a> and <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-t005" class="usa-link">Table 5</a> (cf. MA values at 0.9 V vs. RHE) decreased in the following order: Pt/C &gt; Pt/50GNP-S2 &gt; Pt/100GNP-S2 &gt; Pt/75GNP-S2 &gt; Pt/25GNP-S2 &gt; Pt100GO. In addition, the MA results obtained at 0.85 V and 0.9 V on the Pt/Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C electrocatalysts with Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>/C = 75/25 mass ratio prepared in this work and in ref. [<a href="#B18-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-nanomaterials-15-01753">18</a>] are also visualized in <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S15</a>. As shown in <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S15</a>, between the catalysts synthesized in this work, the highest activity in the ORR was obtained on the Pt/50GNP-S2 and Pt/100GNP-S2 samples; the best activity among other catalysts with a mass ratio of Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>/C = 75/25 presented in ref. [<a href="#B18-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-nanomaterials-15-01753">18</a>] was obtained on the GNP-NG-containing catalyst.</p>
<p>Current density and MA obtained in the ORR at different potentials on the Pt/Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C catalytic systems with different Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>/C mass ratios are compared in <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Table S8</a> in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a>. In <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Table S8</a>, the results obtained on Pt/Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C electrocatalysts with a Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>/C ratio of 75/25 were compared with the results presented in our recent study on electrocatalysts with higher carbonaceous materials content [<a href="#B90-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B90-nanomaterials-15-01753">90</a>]. As follows from <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Table S8</a>, among other catalysts with a Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>/C mass ratio of 25/75, the catalyst containing 75 wt.% of Vulcan XC-72 was the most promising.</p>
<p>As shown in <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Table S8</a>, the mass activity of the Pt/Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C (C: functionalized Black Pearls 2000 (FBP)) catalyst with Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>/C = 50/50 mass ratio reported in [<a href="#B75-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B75-nanomaterials-15-01753">75</a>] was comparable with the activity of both the GNP-containing catalysts with lower carbon content (Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>/C = 75/25). The reductive pretreatment resulted in a significant increase in the MA values. In this series of experiments, the highest MA was observed in the catalyst reduced at 250 °C (Pt/FBP-250H); only this catalyst reached the mass activity of the commercial Pt/C.</p>
<p>In conclusion, it should be noted that the catalytic systems presented in this study contain too much oxide in their composition (Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>/C = 75/25 mass ratio) to be used as a cathode. As is known, the load of the electrocatalyst on the cathode side, due to the slow kinetics of the oxygen reduction reaction, is approximately three times higher than on the anode side. Therefore, high oxide content typically causes increased resistance and mass transfer limitations, resulting in low activity. In this regard, GNP-GO mixture-containing catalytic systems may be of practical interest for use as a cathode only in the case of using catalysts with a lower content of mixed oxide (e.g., Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>/C ratio of 25/75). Furthermore, based on the previous results, reduction of the most promising catalysts to create strong metal-support interactions may be the next strategy to enhance the activity of the oxygen reduction reaction.</p>
<p>As shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f012" class="usa-link">Figure 12</a>, as expected, fairly close values of the diffusion-limited current are observed for the reference Pt/C and all GNP-containing catalysts. The only exception is the result obtained for the Pt/100GO catalyst, apparently indicating the difference in the support morphology and/or structural characteristics of the support. It should be noted that in oxide-containing Pt catalysts, the lower <em>j</em><sub>lim</sub> can also be attributed to reversible oxide formation/reduction on Pt [<a href="#B91-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B91-nanomaterials-15-01753">91</a>] and to slower oxygen diffusion through the oxide layer covering the Pt nanoparticles [<a href="#B92-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B92-nanomaterials-15-01753">92</a>,<a href="#B93-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B93-nanomaterials-15-01753">93</a>].</p>
<p>As can be seen in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f012" class="usa-link">Figure 12</a>A, very similar diffusion-limited currents were obtained on the Pt/100GNP-S2, Pt/50GNP-S2 and commercial Pt/C electrocatalysts, while the limiting current of the other two GNP-containing catalysts was slightly lower. Since, according to the Koutecký–Levich equation, in the presence of an ideal non-porous catalyst layer on the working electrode, the diffusion-limited current density (<em>j</em><sub>lim</sub>) depends only on the rotation rate, thus it should theoretically be the same. In this regard, we should emphasize that the difference in the <em>j</em><sub>lim</sub> observed in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f012" class="usa-link">Figure 12</a> on the GNP-containing catalysts was less than 10%. Note that, according to Mayrhofer et al. [<a href="#B74-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B74-nanomaterials-15-01753">74</a>], for carbon-containing supported catalysts, this difference in the diffusion-limited current values observed in the ORR measurements is within the expected relative measurement errors. As shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f012" class="usa-link">Figure 12</a>B, representing ORR activity in units of mass activity only slightly affects the order of the <em>j</em><sub>lim</sub> decrease: in this case, the highest limiting current was observed on the Pt/100GNP-S2 and Pt/75GNP-S2 catalysts.</p>
<p>In additional experiments, the ORR measurements were carried out before and after the 500-cycle stability test. <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f012" class="usa-link">Figure 12</a> compares the ORR catalytic activity obtained before and after the short-term stability test of the commercial Pt/C and Mo-containing composite-supported Pt electrocatalysts at 1600 rpm. As shown in <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f012" class="usa-link">Figure 12</a>, after 500 cycles, only minor changes in the polarization curves were observed for all the catalysts studied.</p>
<p><a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S16</a> in the <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials</a> compares the mass activity values at 0.9 V for both the commercial reference Pt/C and the catalysts synthesized in this work calculated for the ORR measurements conducted before and after stability tests. As can be seen from <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S16</a>, the MA obtained on the Pt/75GNP-S2 and Pt/25GNP-S2 catalytic systems during the ORR carried out after the 500-cycle stability test was higher compared to the result obtained in the first ORR experiment. This behavior may be due to changes in the surface structure of the catalysts after the first ORR experiment.</p>
<p>As illustrated in <a href="#app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link">Figure S16</a>, the catalysts synthesized in this work exhibit lower initial mass activities than the commercial Pt/C, which is expected given that Pt/C serves as a well-optimized benchmark catalyst with highly uniform dispersion and controlled particle size distribution.</p>
<p>It should be noted that these results represent only short-term behavior; the 500-cycle polarization test is insufficient to draw definitive conclusions about long-term stability. Indeed, as we demonstrated above (see <a href="#nanomaterials-15-01753-f010" class="usa-link">Figure 10</a>) and in our previous study conducted on Pt/Ti<sub>0.9</sub>Sn<sub>0.1</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C catalysts, after 2500 cycles the rate of activity decay for the composite-supported catalysts becomes significantly lower than that of Pt/C, indicating better durability under prolonged operation [<a href="#B78-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B78-nanomaterials-15-01753">78</a>].</p>
<p>In conclusion, it should be noted that among the Mo-containing electrocatalysts, the best and similar activity in the ORR was obtained on the Pt/50GNP-S2 and Pt/100GNP-S2 electrocatalysts. Moreover, in this series of experiments, the Pt/50GNP-S2 catalyst demonstrated the highest long-term stability (ΔECSA<sub>10,000</sub> = 21.2%), comparable to the best results we had previously obtained on Pt/Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>-C catalytic systems. In addition, this catalyst with a GNPs/GO ratio of 50/50 had an increased CO tolerance, similar to that of the Pt/100GO.</p>
<p>Thus, based on the obtained results, it can be stated that the use of the GNPs-GO mixture with a GNPs/GO = 50/50 ratio as carbon material in the preparation of a composite support allows us to combine the beneficial properties of GNPs and GO, which leads to high activity and stability of the resulting catalyst.</p></section></section><section id="sec4-nanomaterials-15-01753"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Conclusions</h2>
<p>In conclusion, 20 wt.% Pt/(75 wt.% Ti<sub>0.8</sub>Mo<sub>0.2</sub>O<sub>2</sub>—25 wt.% C)-type composite-supported electrocatalysts were successfully prepared from GNP and GNPs-GO mixtures. According to XRD measurements, mixed oxide existed in the desired rutile phase in all samples.</p>
<p>Regarding the results of the physicochemical characterization of the sample series with varying GO content, it can be concluded that both the large-scale texture of the composite-supported catalysts and the functional group content of the carbonaceous component of their support reflected the increasing presence of GO beside GNPs. It facilitates formation of a favorable, more dispersed oxide layer, similarly to the use of functionalized carbonaceous materials. The electrical conductivity values were significantly higher when the GNPs-GO mixtures were used as the starting carbon material of the composite, compared to the use of pure GNPs, indicating a percolating carbonaceous network in the composite.</p>
<p>In the case of all the composite-supported electrocatalysts, increased CO oxidation activity was achieved due to the incorporated Mo, and the stability of the composite-supported Pt catalysts was significantly higher than that of commercial Pt/C. Furthermore, the increased stability of GNPs-GO mixture-containing catalyst compared to the GO- and GNP-derived mixtures was observed. Based on these results, we believe that introduction of the GO component improved the surface chemical properties of the GNP-based hard template due to its functional groups, which were partially retained after the high-temperature treatment phase of the composite synthesis and can serve as anchoring sites for Pt nanoparticles.</p></section><section id="ack1" class="ack"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Acknowledgments</h2>
<p>The authors thank István E. Sajó for their help in the evaluation of the XRD results and Miklós Mohai Jr. for the ICP-OES measurements.</p></section><section id="app1-nanomaterials-15-01753" class="app"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Materials</h2>
